# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-28

| **[VibeVideoBench:Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a ruleconsistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, judged by three VLM judge panels and validated with blind human preferences. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, the benchmark is far from solved: GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 lead clearly, yet even the best system fails a core requirement on about a quarter of tasks. Whether a video arrives at all matters as much as its quality, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders started too late, run too slowly or never started. The judges agree with each other and with human preferences on system rankings, but only moderately on individual videos.</p></details> |  |
| **[Work Agents: Systems, Capabilities, and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35845v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As foundation models evolve from information generation to digital work execution, agents are increasingly capable of completing complex tasks across tools, applications, and workflows. However, existing research lacks a unified perspective on how agents reliably accomplish delegated work. To address this gap, we present a work-centric, system-level perspective that views work agents as systems combining foundation models and runtime harnesses to execute tasks in stateful workspaces and deliver verifiable outcomes. We introduce two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness, to characterize working conditions, and classify digital work into five forms according to task scope and organization. Building on this framework, we identify five core capability families required for reliable work execution and examine approaches to model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. We further analyze existing evaluation methods and metrics from the perspective of work outcomes. Finally, we discuss future research directions, including sustained work delegation, adaptation to open environments, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. This paper provides a unified framework for understanding, optimizing, and evaluating work agents, supporting the development of systems capable of delivering reliable, verifiable, and practically valuable work outcomes.</p></details> |  |
| **[ACARE: Agent-Context-Aware Reranker for Iterative Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35840v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In agentic search, a document can match an agent-generated sub-query without helping answer the user's original question. Conventional rerankers score documents against the sub-query alone, overlooking the broader task and the agent's reasoning. We introduce the Agent-Context-Aware Reranker (ACARe), a compact 4B LLM trained to rerank documents using this context when available. Across multiple benchmarks and agent families, ACARe improves retrieval quality over its base reranker while reducing search effort. These benefits extend to answer generation: ACARe improves accuracy and reduces searches over no reranking, while matching or exceeding the base reranker's mean accuracy with more precise evidence. It also helps smaller agents narrow the retrieval-quality gap to larger agents, showing how better retrieval tools can make smaller agents more effective. The model, training data, and code will be released soon.</p></details> |  |
| **[Profiling a Frontier Agent with EBench: From Direct Control to Harness with Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35832v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can control robots without robot-specific training, but how their capabilities compare and interact with post-trained vision-language-action (VLA) and world action model (WAM) policies across manipulation demands remains to be thoroughly evaluated. In this report, we evaluate GPT-6-Astra on EBench as the core of an agent that controls a robot through an agent harness under the same visual and proprioceptive observation protocol as post-trained policies. We study two agent configurations: direct control, in which the agent generates waypoint commands directly, and harness with policy, in which it can also invoke a post-trained VLA or WAM policy for action execution. Under direct control, the agent achieves competitive overall performance relative to post-trained policies. It leads on short-horizon tasks and exhibits the least performance variation across generalization dimensions, whereas it underperforms post-trained policies on long-horizon tasks, tabletop manipulation, and high-precision contact-rich tasks. In the harness with policy, invoking a post-trained VLA or WAM combines the complementary strengths of both components, raising overall task success to the level of the strongest compared post-trained policies while substantially reducing token cost. However, performance remains below the per-task upper bound of the standalone components due to suboptimal policy invocation, fixed-instruction execution without subgoals, and grasp disruption when the agent resumes control. Overall, our study characterizes the capability boundary of a frontier MLLM agent and suggests directions for improving future agent harness design. Project page: https://internrobotics.github.io/EBench-Astra-Tech-Report/</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://internrobotics.github.io/EBench-Astra-Tech-Report/</p></details> |
| **[Back to Basics: A Skill-Centric Approach to Generalist Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35823v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Browser agents do not just need more practice. They need to practice the right things. We introduce MiniWeb, a realistic miniature of the web comprising 65 interconnected websites across 15 domains, where agents navigate, search, and transact as people do. Its 369 human-annotated tasks cover everyday workflows and label 37 reusable interaction primitives grouped into 7 skill families, allowing us to diagnose which skills fail rather than only whether a task succeeds. Benchmarking ten open-weight agents, we find that drag & gesture is consistently the weakest family, with no agent solving more than 24% of its instances. To address this gap, we introduce MiniWebGen, a framework that autonomously generates verified, skill-centric training data from MiniWeb. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-4B on just 3.1K verified trajectories improves single-primitive success by 24.9pp across 13 unseen websites, with gains across every skill family. However, these trajectories teach short-horizon interactions, which do not directly translate to full-task success. We build on this result with MiniWebAgent, which pairs the trained executor with a planner that decomposes full tasks into the skill-level steps it learned to perform. This approach improves task success by 19.4pp points on unseen MiniWeb sites and transfers to external benchmarks, with gains of 9.3pp on WebArena-Lite, 16.1pp on Online-Mind2Web, and 20.0pp on WebVoyager, without training on their trajectories. We provide a simple recipe for compact browser agents: practice reusable skills directly, then compose them to solve unfamiliar tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization in a Sim-Trained Robot Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot policy needs to understand what a task is asking for in a scene, and how to move the robot to accomplish that task. To achieve these two skills, we introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches a small off-the-shelf vision-language model to control a robot via a simple tool harness. We use a pretrained 27B parameter model to bootstrap world understanding and reasoning, followed by supervised fine-tuning and GRPO on simple simulated tasks to teach it how to move the robot. Without any real-world robot data during training, ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness and outperforming the MolmoAct2 VLA (33.3%), while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. We demonstrate that ZAP also generalizes to completely new objects, goals, strategies, and language by seating a test tube in a rack (and choosing a different hole in the rack when prompted), handing a test tube between arms mid-air, drawing an X on a tic-tac-toe board, and playing chess. These goals and types of manipulation don't appear in our training data. Furthermore, ZAP also transfers to an unseen embodiment. On a single-arm Franka, ZAP reaches 55.9% on RoboLab-120, 4.9 percentage points ahead of the leading VLA/WAM baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35801v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($λ$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).</p></details> | 32 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Quantifying Claim Smoothing by LLM Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35793v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research, or any use of large language models (LLMs) to read a large document collection and produce insights, requires synthesizing information from different sources. In this work, we find that LLMs produce long-form research reports that read as confident syntheses but which fail to accurately reflect the underlying documents. We call this failure smoothing. In collapsing diverse and potentially conflicting sources into a single narrative, the model discards the evidential "jaggedness" of its corpus and asserts conclusions more decisive than the citations can support. We introduce a claim level scoring pipeline, SmoothScore, to quantify this effect. Our pipeline extracts key conclusive claims from model generated reports, then finds evidence relevant to each claim and clusters that evidence along high level aspects. Finally, each claim is scored with respect to the evidence to quantify the degree of smoothing done by the agent. Across 7 systems and 4 datasets, we find that agents consistently produce stronger claims than warranted.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data present at: https://github.com/tiasa2/SmoothScore</p></details> |
| **[A Unified Arena for LLM Agents across Diverse Eurogame](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35773v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive environments offer a practical way to evaluate large language model (LLM) agents, and competitive games extend this evaluation to decisions shaped by competing goals and other agents' choices. Yet existing evaluations confined to individual games or execution frameworks leave unclear which competitive advantages persist across settings. We introduce DECE (Diverse Eurogame Competitive Environments), a unified arena spanning ten Eurogames with shared interfaces and evaluation protocols. These games combine lasting strategic commitments, competition over shared resources, and multiple routes to victory. Our primary study evaluates 12 model-harness configurations, spanning nine models and four execution harnesses, in 480 three-player matches across ten games. The main and supplementary studies comprise 1,032 completed matches in total. These comparisons reveal broad cross-game ranking agreement alongside game-specific strengths. Matched studies show that the same model can perform differently across execution harnesses, adding another source of variation to these profiles. These performance advantages do not consistently translate into better token or cost efficiency, making resource use an additional consideration when comparing configurations. Together, the findings support evaluating complete agent configurations by both their competitive performance across games and the resources needed to achieve it.</p></details> |  |
| **[Free to Look, Free to Reason: Simple LLM Harnesses Outdiagnose Hand-Crafted Root Cause Analysis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35767v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Root cause analysis (RCA) of microservice failures has attracted many LLM agents whose investigation is hand-crafted, through standard operating procedures, fixed stages and role agents. Each is evaluated on its own data against few baselines, so it is unknown whether this design beats letting a capable model investigate on its own. We compare three simple harnesses that prescribe none of the model's calls (one call over an evidence package, a SQL agent and the Codex coding agent) with five published LLM RCA agents and with statistical and causal methods on RCAtlas, 90 validated cases from eight public datasets, across six models and three reasoning efforts. The simple harnesses are the most accurate (PR@1 65-77% on three mid-tier models), ahead of the published agents (16-53%) and the non-LLM methods (6-39%), on every model on which both families were run. The published agents fix from a tenth to all of their model calls in the program, and a case study shows how these prescriptions skip the decisive evidence. A stronger model mainly improves reliability, a multi-turn harness mainly improves aptitude, and more reasoning effort changes accuracy by a few points at most while multiplying the time by up to seven. Among runs more accurate than the best non-LLM method, every Pareto-optimal run in cost or time uses a simple harness.</p></details> | 12 pages |
| **[Preference-Guided Cooperation in Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35736v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MOMARL) addresses agent coordination and objective trade-offs simultaneously while optimising overall system performance. Existing work tends to treat multi-agent cooperation and multi-objective optimisation as largely independent components of a learning problem, which amounts to an exorbitant complexity except in trivial cases. We study here whether the structure provided by multiple objectives can itself be exploited to facilitate cooperation, rather than being treated as an additional optimisation challenge. We formulate MOMARL at both the team and individual levels and introduce objective-wise counterfactual credit assignment to evaluate each agent's contribution across the team objectives. We propose Meta-Gradient Informed Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MIRL), with static (MIRL-S) and dynamic (MIRL-D) variants. By incorporating meta-gradient information, MIRL enables agents to learn how to prioritise objectives individually while collectively optimising the system-level objective, allowing the multi-objective structure to directly guide cooperation. Experiments on a multi-objective multiple particle environment show that our methods improve Pareto-front quality over baselines, with the dynamic variant achieving the strongest performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 4 figures, 1 table (plus 3 figures, 3 tables, and 2 algorithms in the appendix)</p></details> |
| **[Automated Standardization of Legacy Biomedical Metadata Using an Ontology-Constrained LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08552v5)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Descriptive scientific metadata in public repositories are often incomplete and inconsistent with community standards and ontologies, limiting data FAIRness. Large language models (LLM) offer a promising approach to automatically standardizing such metadata when the LLM is provided with knowledge of the relevant standards in machine-actionable form. Entry of metadata standards through prompt engineering offers only fixed snapshots of the relevant standards, however, and it relies on an LLM's pretrained knowledge to interpret and satisfy constraints related to the standard. We evaluate whether giving an LLM access to metadata specifications and authoritative terminology at runtime improves automated metadata standardization. We present ARMS, a tool-using LLM agent that retrieves complete CEDAR metadata templates and that queries authoritative biomedical terminology services dynamically at execution time. We compared ARMS with a prompt-engineering approach on 839 legacy metadata records describing experiment datasets with expert-standardized records serving as the reference standard. Across the corpus, ARMS outperformed the prompt-engineering approach in converting legacy metadata to standards-compliant form, increasing precision from 0.56 to 0.93 and recall from 0.51 to 0.85, with improvements across all field categories and assay types. The largest gains occurred for ontology-constrained fields where precision increased from 0.36 to 0.92. Our findings show that access to appropriate metadata standards can substantially improve LLM-based standardization of legacy metadata. ARMS improves metadata standardization by giving LLMs runtime access to the authoritative resources that define valid metadata. Representation of metadata standards in machine-actionable form enhances the task of rectifying legacy metadata using LLM technology, especially when those standards can be queried dynamically.</p></details> |  |
| **[MiRL: Error-Targeted Reinforcement Learning for Correcting Terminal Agent Misbehaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35697v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Error-targeted learning gives a legible way to improve a model: identify specific weaknesses of the model, train the model to fix these weaknesses, and check whether the weaknesses are fixed. We study how to apply error-targeted learning to terminal agents, which solve long-horizon tasks specified in natural language by interacting with a command shell. Existing error-targeted methods are designed for single-turn tasks, and existing analyses of agent failures are used to evaluate and debug agents rather than to train them. We propose Misbehavior-driven RL (MiRL), which first profiles the errors of a policy to find generalizable misbehaviors: kinds of wrong steps that recur across many environments. MiRL then trains the policy with RL, starting rollouts at the steps where these misbehaviors occur and rewarding a rollout only if it passes the tests and avoids the misbehavior. On Qwen3.5-4B, eight misbehaviors account for 52.1% of wrong steps. After training with MiRL, the policy repeats the measured misbehaviors 43% to 57% less often on average on unseen prefixes. MiRL outperforms TMax-4B on Terminal-Bench Lite (51.8% vs. 47.4%) and matches it on Terminal-Bench 2.1 (21.3% vs. 20.6%), while using 35% of its rollouts. These results show that error-targeted learning can be applied to realistic, long-horizon terminal tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 14 figures, 5 tables. Project page: https://github.com/runchu-tian/MiRL</p></details> |
| **[MicroAgent: Context-Augmented Multi-Agent Framework for Automatic Microservice Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29742v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The adoption of Microservice Architecture (MSA) has revolutionized software engineering by enhancing scalability, agility, and maintainability over traditional monolithic applications. As more developers transition their legacy systems to microservice-based architectures, effective microservice decomposition-partitioning monolithic applications into highly cohesive services-becomes vital. However, this decomposition task presents significant challenges. Manual approaches are time-consuming and labor-intensive. Existing automated methods often fail to capture the necessary semantic insights from complex applications, while naive applications of Large Language Models tend to overlook crucial contextual information and design principles, leading to suboptimal results. To address these challenges, we propose MicroAgent, a Context-Augmented Multi-Agent Framework for Microservice Decomposition. Our framework divides the decomposition process into five distinct subtasks and assigns each to a specialized agent. To enhance the effectiveness of each agent, we provide tailored, multi-granularity context that keeps its analysis focused and mitigates information overload. Furthermore, to ensure the decomposition adheres to established design principles, we integrate analytical tools that guide the agents' decision-making. Experimental evaluations on 10 Java Web applications demonstrate that MicroAgent achieves an average decomposition accuracy of 89.2%, outperforming the state-of-the-art method by 24.6%. We also conduct a case study to highlight the practical benefits of our design.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 41st IEEE/ACM International Conference on Automated Software Engineering (ASE 2026)</p></details> |
| **[EvoRouter: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent Routing via Insight-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35690v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Serving web queries at scale with multi-agent systems requires jointly configuring large language models, collaboration modes, agent counts, interaction rounds, and skills. However, the resulting configuration space grows combinatorially, while sparse execution rewards provide limited fine-grained supervision for interdependent planning decisions. We propose EvoRouter, a self-evolving multi-agent routing framework driven by insight-guided on-policy distillation. A dedicated expert periodically synthesizes performance-cost feedback from configuration trajectories across tasks into reusable conditional routing insights about capability requirements, returns from resource scaling, and interactions among configuration choices. A privileged teacher combines these evolving insights with current execution feedback to provide dense token-level supervision for the router's own planning trajectories through on-policy distillation. Cost-aware reinforcement learning jointly optimizes task performance and computational cost. Together, these training signals internalize execution insights into the routing policy. The updated policy generates new trajectories that support subsequent insight extraction, forming an execution-driven self-improvement loop. We evaluate EvoRouter on 19 in-domain datasets spanning five domains and two out-of-domain benchmarks. EvoRouter achieves an average in-domain accuracy of 68.69%, outperforming the strongest routing baseline by 7.23 percentage points while reducing inference compute cost by 50.95%. Our code is open-sourced at https://github.com/CenYangtze/EvoRouter.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 12 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[Verse: Semantics and Verification of Hybrid Multi-Agent Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35680v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Verifying cyber-physical systems with many interacting agents remains difficult because interactions are mediated by sensors, individual decisions are constrained by environment (map) structure, and the resulting mode space scales combinatorially. We present Verse, a framework for writing agents, maps, and sensors in a familiar programming language syntax, with a formal semantics for the resulting scenarios. A compiler translates each scenario to a hybrid automaton whose executions correspond exactly to the runs of the scenario. Reachability analysis on this automaton builds an execution tree from two post operators, one for continuous flow over a time step and one for a single agent's mode change, and checks safety assertions at every vertex. The tree expands only the modes that are reached, without enumerating the global mode space. Experiments highlight expressive modeling across cars and drones, 2D/3D maps (including OpenDRIVE imports), noisy sensors, and multiple simultaneous safety assertions. Verse verifies a scenario with 7 agents and 37 modes in 90 seconds on a standard laptop; it is competitive with CORA and JuliaReach on ARCH benchmarks, and on multi-agent scenarios is an order of magnitude faster than JuliaReach at the cost of looser reachable sets.</p></details> |  |
| **[CIPHER: A Decoupled Exploration-Selection Framework for Test-Time Scaling of Data Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14386v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise data science and analytics spans closed-ended information extraction with verifiable answers and open-ended insight discovery. Agents are evaluated on one or the other, so there is no evidence on whether one system can serve both. We present \texttt{CIPHER}, a data science agent built on a Decoupled Exploration--Selection (DES) framework that applies test-time scaling at the plan level: generating $N$ candidate analysis plans, selecting $M{<}N$ for parallel execution, and aggregating the results. We evaluate $48$ distinct design choices for the DES framework (spanning generation, selection, budget and aggregator capacity) jointly on $357$ tasks from Infi-DA-Bench (closed-ended) and InsightBench (open-ended), on a proprietary and an open-weights base model. A single zero-tuning configuration is competitive against purpose-built specialist baselines under matched models, within $1.2$pp of \texttt{DataWise} and $1.3$pp of \texttt{Agent-Poirot}. The joint sweep exposes that generation and aggregation are the most influential performance levers, while random selection --- the only strategy with zero overhead --- matches its tuned alternatives. These results offer new insights on the design of test-time scaling strategies, and on how a fixed inference budget is best allocated across them, for agents deployed on enterprise analytics workloads.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented at the 2nd Workshop on Agentic AI Benchmarks and Applications for Enterprise Tasks (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[FinAutoRubric: Expert-Guided Automatic Rubric Generation for Evaluating Financial Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35744v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating finance research agents requires rubrics that reflect expert standards and fix the values correct as of an information cutoff. Expert-reviewed finance benchmarks rely on fixed, per-item rubrics, which are costly to extend and cannot encode each institution's own standard. In FinAutoRubric, experts specify reusable evaluation guidance, while agents and code carry out query-specific rubric generation, review, and validation. This expert guidance governs every agent, as prompts and as rules that code enforces, and a Task Bank of reusable criteria carries it across tasks. In long-horizon loops that follow the expert guidance, a writer agent researches every expected value and a reviewer agent verifies it, and failures escalate to a human. On three expert-authored finance benchmarks, its rubrics track expert scoring as closely as the strongest evaluated generator while stating the expert rubric's expected value for more criteria, their scores agree with human grading, and in-house analysts prefer them in a blind review. The released 100-query FinAutoRubric Benchmark, built from in-house analysts' key questions across 78 tasks and eight asset classes, shows that rubrics from an earlier model generation still leave headroom for a later one.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[Recovering Wasted Compute in Autoresearch Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10424v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A slew of recent works develop agents for solving research problems end-to-end, a paradigm increasingly referred to as autoresearch. Such agents have inspired large industry investment, motivated by their potential to automate time-consuming human labor and customize machine learning solutions for specialized applications. In this paper, we study the modeling pipeline at the core of these autoresearch systems and identify common failure modes when they are applied to tabular datasets: (1) they waste compute resolving the same bugs over and over again; (2) they often fail to tune hyperparameters even when they have a large remaining compute budget; (3) the tree-search algorithms that power them do not explore; and (4) they perform data analysis, mimicking the humans whose data they are trained on, but do not use that analysis to make downstream decisions. We explore targeted interventions and find that a global debug consultant that shares discovered runtime constraints across all branches of the search tree, prompt- and control-level enhancements, and refined tree-search algorithms successfully recover wasted compute. Our results show that large gains in autoresearch agent performance are achievable through agentic design alone, holding the underlying language model fixed.</p></details> |  |
| **[Hierarchical Skill Evolution for Coding Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35616v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Skills provide agents with reusable experience to guide future decisions without modifying the underlying model. However, coding trajectories are often entangled with task- and repository-specific contexts, making it challenging to distill procedural skills that generalize across diverse coding environments. To address this challenge, we present Hierarchical Skill Evolution (HSE), which progressively distills procedural knowledge from coding trajectories through hierarchical evidence aggregation. HSE first extracts task-level evidence from individual trajectories, then aggregates recurring evidence within the same repository to form repository-specific skill candidates, and finally consolidates consistent patterns across repositories into transferable skills across repositories. The evolution is driven by an external writer-evaluator loop that generates and assesses skills while keeping the underlying coding model frozen. The loop externally updates the writer and evaluator policies to improve subsequent skill generation and assessment. This hierarchy progressively filters task-specific details while preserving procedures that generalize across diverse coding environments. Successful trajectories yield reusable guidance for future tasks, while failed trajectories reveal recurring failure modes that guide avoidance and recovery. We evaluate HSE on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE, using GLM-5.2 across Pi and Claude Code harnesses and GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8 with Pi. Across both benchmarks, 10 skills evolved solely from SWE-Gym improve coding performance across all evaluated settings, demonstrating transfer across benchmarks, coding models, and agent harnesses. We further evolve the skill library from target-benchmark trajectories without verifier feedback, relying solely on the learned evaluator for candidate assessment and achieving additional gains through benchmark-tailored skills.</p></details> |  |
| **[KAPPA: Checking the Proofs in Statistics Papers with Lean 4 and Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35596v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Papers in statistical theory rest on long proofs that few readers check line by line. We present KAPPA (Kit of Agents for Proof-checking Papers with a proof Assistant), open-source software that organizes language model agents, run by Claude Code, to translate the proofs of a LaTeX paper into the proof assistant Lean 4 with its library Mathlib and check them there, result by result. Each numbered result receives one of four verdicts: verified unconditionally; verified conditionally, with every cited or unproved input stated as a named hypothesis; issue recorded, with its place in the paper and the side at fault; or unfinished, with the reason. The kit follows the paper's own proof step by step, rather than finding a new one. Separate agents write and review, each statement is compared with the paper before it is proved and fingerprinted afterwards, and every verdict rests on evidence that can be rerun without a language model. The kit runs unattended for hours, waits out usage limits, re-checks only what a revision changed and reports its cost by role, model and result. We describe the design, walk through a complete run, and apply the kit to four example papers with answer keys and to three published statistical papers, checked blind in their versions from before public corrections. In all three, the kit found the published error at the step that the correction names, together with further problems that we confirmed by hand. Given the same papers directly, the same language model working with Lean classified fewer results correctly on the longest paper and reported some statements narrower than the paper's as proved; no verdict of the kit claimed more than its Lean evidence showed.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 2 figures. Software: https://github.com/ziwaa-se/kappa</p></details> |
| **[Agentic Algorithm Design: LLMs that Propose, Formalise, and Prove Runtime Bounds for Evolutionary Algorithms in Lean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35595v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs now prove hard theorems in Lean. Can they also design algorithms and prove their correctness and efficiency? LLM-guided design of optimisation heuristics (such as AlphaEvolve) is purely empirical. Automated theorem provers with LLMs prove given statements. So far, there has been little evidence that LLMs can do both: design an algorithm and guarantee its efficiency by a mathematical proof. We present a staged pipeline modelled after research methodology in design and analysis of evolutionary algorithms. The agent proposes black-box optimisation algorithms, and Lean guards filter out flawed designs. The agent then defines a potential function and proves drift bounds that guarantee an expected runtime improvement over the baseline algorithm. | **[Scaling Verifiable Environments for Long-horizon Work Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04906v5)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Work agents operate over digital artifacts to execute professional knowledge-intensive work, requiring training environments that support long-horizon interaction and trustworthy verification. | **[Testing with Coding Agents: A Large-Scale Comparison of Agent-Supported and Human-Authored Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35297v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Context. Coding agents increasingly contribute tests through pull requests that developers then maintain, so how testing work is divided affects long-term maintainability. Objective. We compare pull requests in which developers and coding agents collaborate (agent-supported) with pull requests without detectable agent involvement (human-authored) along five dimensions: the frequency and extent of test changes, their type and context, test structure, test smells, and code coverage. Pooled across repositories, both groups change test code almost equally often; within repositories, the odds that an agent-supported pull request does so are 1.65 times higher. Agent-supported pull requests add tests more often (odds ratios 1.50-1.58) and modify (0.70-0.76) and delete (0.51-0.77) them less often. As Artificial Intelligence progresses towards Artificial General Intelligence, the multiverse of games has evolved from mere entertainment into the ultimate ground for training and evaluating AGI. Taken together, our work offers a unified lens onto a rapidly shifting field, and a principled path toward the omnipotent generalist agent capable of seamlessly mastering any challenge within the multiverse of games, thereby paving the way for AGI.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://thusi-lab.github.io/Awesome-LFMs-Play-Games/ , Github: https://github.com/THUSI-Lab/Awesome-LFMs-Play-Games</p></details> |

| **[Matcha: Multi-Stage Riemannian Flow Matching for Accurate and Physically Valid Molecular Docking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14586v4)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting a protein--ligand binding pose is central to structure-based drug design, yet current docking methods still struggle to combine speed, accuracy and physical plausibility. Across the benchmarks considered, Matcha attains the strongest physical plausibility and runs about $28\times$ faster than modern large-scale co-folding models. Code and benchmarking artifacts are available at https://github.com/LigandPro/Matcha and archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23568093. | **[MLIPDiff: Guiding Denoising Diffusion Models for Structure-Based Drug Design via Machine-Learned Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33315v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion models have become a dominant approach to structure-based drug design. However, because they are trained primarily to fit reference structures, they often place ligands in physically implausible poses that clash with the protein. We introduce MLIPDiff, an inference-time guidance method that can be applied to pretrained diffusion models for ligand generation. The method uses physics-informed guidance from UMA (Universal Models for Atoms), a machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP). The guidance combines four weighted energy terms (protein-ligand interaction, ligand strain, steric clash, and pocket anchoring) and uses the original model checkpoint without retraining. On CrossDocked2020, MLIPDiff completely eliminates protein-ligand collisions while preserving the ligand's local geometry, improving bond-length distributions and physical plausibility relative to the base model. Compared to models such as NucleusDiff that are specifically designed to reduce atomic clashes, MLIPDiff achieves clash-free structures without compromising local geometry. It maintains the base model's PoseBusters pass rate and attains the best median Vina Score, Vina Min, and Vina Dock, surpassing both the base model and NucleusDiff, with a median of 59.6\% of ligands per pocket binding more strongly than the reference. MLIPDiff thus addresses a limitation of prior methods by generating physically plausible structures with favorable predicted binding affinity using a single frozen model and no additional training.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |

Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. | **[Empowering LLMs for Structure-Based Drug Design via Exploration-Augmented Latent Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15333v2)** | 2026-01-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) possess strong representation and reasoning capabilities, but their application to structure-based drug design (SBDD) is limited by insufficient understanding of protein structures and unpredictable molecular generation. ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[An Interactive Evolutionary Multiobjective Optimization Algorithm Based on Interpretable Active Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35470v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many decision-making tasks are inherently multi-objective. Interactive evolutionary multi-objective optimization algorithms (iEMOAs) are particularly well-suited to help the decision-maker find their most preferred solution. However, many of these algorithms employ black-box preference models. For high-stakes decision-making tasks, like healthcare and defense, a high level of transparency is needed, for instance to support accountability. We propose an interpretable iEMOA which uses active learning to efficiently learn a tree-based preference model directly from pairwise comparisons. The survival operator of NSGA-II and NSGA-III is extended with the learned preference model to guide the algorithm towards the region of interest. An extensive experimental study compares the proposed method to a state-of-the-art black-box model and a state-of-the-art interpretable model. Results indicate that our proposed model outperforms the alternatives in terms of finding the most preferred solution at the cost of a slightly worse average final population. Finally, a case study on a real-life engineering problem illustrates how inspecting the learned model reveals which objectives drive the decision-maker's preferences.</p></details> | 20 pages, 15 figures |
| **[KubePACS: Kubernetes Cluster Using Performant, Highly Available, and Cost Efficient Spot Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24027v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud users aim to minimize cost while maximizing performance by selecting the most suitable instance types for their workloads. To reduce expenses, spot instances have been widely adopted due to their steep discounts compared to on-demand pricing. However, their use introduces reliability risks due to potential interruptions, and existing research has primarily focused on mitigating this trade-off from a cost or availability perspective alone. Despite the diversity in hardware capabilities among instance types, current provisioning systems tend to ignore performance variation, selecting nodes solely based on minimum resource requirements. In this paper, we present KubePACS, a Kubernetes-native spot instance provisioning system that constructs node pools optimized for both cost and performance while guaranteeing high availability. KubePACS formulates the node selection process as a multi-objective optimization problem, incorporating real-time data such as spot prices, performance benchmarks, and availability scores, including the multi-node Spot Placement Score (SPS). It solves this problem efficiently using an Integer Linear Programming (ILP) approach guided by the Golden Section Search (GSS) algorithm to find the optimal configuration. By integrating with the Karpenter node autoscaler, KubePACS jointly optimizes instance-type selection and node scaling decisions within a standard provisioning workflow. KubePACS also adopts a novel heuristic to support workload-specific preferences by scaling performance metrics for specialized instances. Through extensive evaluation across synthetic and real-world workloads, KubePACS demonstrates on average 55.09% and up to 81.06% higher performance per dollar over state-of-the-art solutions such as Karpenter, SpotVerse, and SpotKube, which only reference the spot instance prices and limited availability data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 27th ACM International Middleware Conference (Middleware 2026)</p></details> |

First, we adopt a two-step SDP formulation to obtain a robust initialization in the non-convex search space. Next, we incorporate differentiable objectives and constraints into an incremental ALM to jointly handle coupled design constraints during global floorplanning. Finally, an SOCP-based legalization strictly enforces feasibility while further reducing area. Experiments on the FloorSet benchmark show that our method satisfies all constraints with the fastest runtime. Compared with state-of-the-art methods, it reduces wirelength and area by 90\% and 16\% on average, and achieves near-optimal results, with wirelength and area being merely 2\% and 1\% larger than the ground-truth references, respectively.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 7 figures, Accepted by International Conference on Computer-Aided Design (ICCAD) 2026</p></details> |
| **[ReMedI: Inference-Time Refinement and Self-Distillation for Controllable Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32103v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In drug development, lead optimization must improve competing properties, such as increasing permeability and reducing toxicity, while limiting scaffold changes that could compromise the lead's potency. Motivated by the limited search capabilities of zero-shot generation, we develop an efficient Metropolis-Hastings search strategy to better explore the proposal distribution. We propose ReMedI, which uses the molecular language model as a base proposer for inference-time alignment. Given a lead and a set of multi-objective tasks, the model proposes edits conditioned on the current molecule, while Metropolis--Hastings refinement guides sequential edits under an objective for property improvement, preservation, and lead similarity. On both C-MuMOInstruct and SMDD benchmarks, refinement improves frozen base models across three backbones, reaching significantly higher multi-objective optimization success rates compared to baselines. | **[Cautious Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31855v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Partial differential equations (PDEs) are indispensable tools for modeling complex phenomena in physics and engineering. Despite their advantages, PINN training requires multi-objective optimization, which often leads to significant gradient conflicts among boundary, initial, and residual loss terms. The Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient (ConFIG) method addresses these conflicts; however, integrating ConFIG with adaptive optimizers such as Adam introduces a critical vulnerability: the conflict-free direction generated by ConFIG is further processed by the optimizer, and the resulting weight update may no longer be conflict-free with respect to the task gradients. To address this issue, Cautious ConFIG is proposed as a concise three-line extension to the ConFIG algorithm that empirically restores the conflict-freeness guarantee prior to the weight update. The method is evaluated on three PINN benchmarks: the 1D Viscous Burgers' equation, the 1D Nonlinear Schrödinger equation, and the 2D Kovasznay flow. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements over the standard ConFIG baseline. Additionally, the stabilizing effect of cautious masking and its reaction to conflict severity, task number, and optimizer lag are investigated using a simple toy system.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code available at [https://github.com/damiha/CautiousConFIG](https://github.com/damiha/CautiousConFIG)</p></details> |

| **[Tradeoff-Aware Territory Design: A Multi-Objective Decision System for Enterprise Customer-to-Seller Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31701v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise customer journeys are shaped not only by product experiences and marketing, but also by recurring resource-allocation decisions. This algorithm iteratively updates parameters using the Jacobian matrix of a vector-valued objective function, in which each row is the gradient of an individual objective. | **[Communication-Efficient Federated Domain Generalization in Edge Networks via Dynamic Prototype Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25810v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) is a distributed paradigm for Internet of Things (IoT) edge networks. | **[Python Tool for Visualizing Variability of Pareto Fronts over Multiple Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.08852v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hyperparameter optimization is crucial to achieving high performance in deep learning. First, to alleviate high-frequency label jaggedness (sample-level manifold jitter that assigns neighboring embeddings to different classes), GeoTTER integrates local geometric structure into the optimal transport formulation via graph-Laplacian smoothing, a technique grounded in spectral graph theory that enforces neighborhood consistency. Second, to correct coherent angular drift (a low-frequency orientation bias in which large groups of samples share the same angular offset from their true label prototypes), we fuse clustering-guided cost components with a globally adjusted transport cost, achieving a multi-objective optimization that respects both global distribution constraints and latent data structure. With a median improvement of +6.82% compared to zero-shot and +2.13% compared to OTTER, GeoTTER shows robust improvements across a diverse set of benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at AISTATS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the 32nd ACM SIGKDD Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD 2026)</p></details> |

We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04987v5)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. To overcome this, we propose data-driven soft labels that estimate the conditional cell-type distribution for each read, and integrate this scheme into $Syto$, a new modular framework for read-level classification-based deconvolution. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track), poster</p></details> |
| **[Secure Multimodal Federated Optimization via Hierarchical DNA-Based Key Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27644v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated optimization over heterogeneous multimodal data enables distributed institutions to collaboratively optimize a shared model using medical images, genomic profiles, clinical records, and physiological signals without centralizing raw data. However, protecting modality-specific data throughout the optimization lifecycle remains insufficiently explored, particularly when heterogeneous modalities require separate encryption keys and selective local access. This paper proposes a secure multimodal federated optimization framework that separates local data protection from federated-update protection. A trusted key-management authority securely provisions one client key through Hybrid Public Key Encryption (HPKE), from which each client locally derives modality-specific symmetric keys using the domain-separated HMAC-based Extract-and-Expand Key Derivation Function (HKDF). Heterogeneous modalities are mapped into a unified DNA-domain representation and processed through a DNA-origami-inspired structural transformation before authenticated encryption with AES-GCM. During local optimization, only the required modalities are decrypted within an authorized client environment. Individual model updates are independently protected through secure aggregation, allowing the coordinator to observe only aggregated updates rather than each client's contribution. Together, these components establish a structured approach to modality-specific protection and secure collaborative optimization, integrating hierarchical key management, authenticated local encryption, and aggregation-level update protection within a unified framework.</p></details> |  |

One hypothetical implementation can use CRISPR-Cas9 and gRNA reagents for labeling. Various aspects of the general labeling channel, including fixed-length labels, are explored, and upper bounds on the maximum size of the corresponding codes are given. The study includes the development of an efficient encoder-decoder pair that is proven to be optimal in terms of maximum code size under specific conditions. The codewords for this framework are codesets, i.e., each codeword is a collection of a few labels. Of special interest are codes whose codesets contain fixed-length labels. Their construction and analysis are tied to de Bruijn sequences and constrained coding.</p></details> |  |

It uses molecules as information carriers and enables communication between nanodevices. DNA-based molecular communication is a promising approach for medical applications, such as in-body networks for diagnostics and drug delivery. However, the transition from theoretical models to practical experiments is still in its early stages. This survey reviews experimentally validated DNA-tiles, nanocarriers, and biosensors relevant to implementing DNA-tile-based nanonetworks in vitro. We evaluate these components based on their stability, compatibility, cost-effectiveness, and overall suitability for wet-lab implementation. Based on this analysis, we identify promising component combinations and derive a blueprint for a minimal proof-of-concept experiment. The principle is domain-agnostic: any measurable position- or context-specific corruption profile - FFPE, bisulfite, metagenomic, or nanopore - substitutes directly for the PMD array, making VESTIGE a knowledge-guided training routine for intelligent systems operating on degraded or noisy sequence inputs.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 9 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Coded Information Retrieval for Block-Structured DNA-Based Data Storage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17154v2)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study the problem of coded information retrieval for block-structured data, motivated by DNA-based storage systems where a database is partitioned into multiple files that must each be recoverable as an atomic unit. The objective is to characterize the set of achievable expected retrieval time pairs $\bigl(E_1(G), E_2(G)\bigr)$ over all $[n,k]$ linear codes with generator matrix $G$. We derive a family of linear lower bounds via mutual exclusivity of recovery sets and develop a nonlinear geometric bound via column projection that holds for every linear code. For codes with no mixed columns, this yields the hyperbolic constraint $s_1/E_1 + s_2/E_2 \le 1$, which we conjecture to hold universally whenever $\max\{s_1,s_2\} \ge 2$. We analyze explicit codes, such as the identity code, file-dedicated MDS codes, and the systematic global MDS code, and compute their exact expected retrieval times. For file-dedicated codes, we prove MDS optimality within the family and verify the hyperbolic constraint. For global MDS codes, we establish dominance by the proportional local MDS allocation via a convex-ordering argument for hypergeometric distributions, simplifying and extending prior work to the asymmetric case. Finally, we characterize the limiting achievability region as $n \to \infty$: the hyperbolic boundary is asymptotically achieved by file-dedicated MDS codes, and is conjectured to be the exact boundary of the limiting achievability region.</p></details> |  |
| **[DNA: Dual-stage Native Attribution for Generated Image Source Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13685v1)** | 2026-07-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid evolution of image generation has produced numerous within-family variants, making source-model attribution of suspect images increasingly important for digital forensics. The coarse-grained stage uses Autoencoder Double-Reconstruction (AEDR) for efficient open-set family-level screening. The fine-grained stage performs closed-set model-level attribution with Native Prediction Consistency (NPC), which compares native prediction errors of within-family variants across multiple noise levels under semantic conditioning and attributes the source via normalized calibrated scores. | **[Preserve to Expand: Source-Aware Adaptation and Model Merging for Multilingual LLM-Based ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35846v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expanding multilingual ASR systems to new languages is challenging when adaptation must preserve previously supported languages without replaying source-language training data. In single-target expansion, target-only fine-tuning and standard TIES merging learn new languages but cause severe source-language forgetting, while SSU-Wanda+TIES gives the best source-preserving trade-off. In multi-target expansion, update merging provides stable source retention, whereas recursive expansion can improve target adaptation under favorable orders but is highly order-sensitive. These results show that multilingual ASR expansion requires balancing target acquisition, source retention, and interactions among target-language updates.</p></details> |  |
| **[CoSpaDi: Compressing LLMs via Calibration-Guided Sparse Dictionary Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22075v7)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training LLM compression often relies on low-rank approximations, which force all columns of a projection matrix to share a single low-dimensional subspace. In collapsing diverse and potentially conflicting sources into a single narrative, the model discards the evidential "jaggedness" of its corpus and asserts conclusions more decisive than the citations can support. We introduce a claim level scoring pipeline, SmoothScore, to quantify this effect. Our pipeline extracts key conclusive claims from model generated reports, then finds evidence relevant to each claim and clusters that evidence along high level aspects. | **[Adversary Brains: Hypothesis-Based Logical Signatures for Threat Actor Detection with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35624v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An adversary brain is an LLM-based model of a threat actor's behavior, held as an evolving logical signature: a hypothesis with enough structure that data can test it continuously, without a person re-interpreting it each time. | **[EF-LLM: A Continual Learning-Based Framework for AI-Assisted Automation, Enhanced Sparse Prediction, and Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00852v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate prediction helps achieve supply-demand balance in energy systems,thereby supporting operational decision-making and scheduling.Traditional models,which lack AI-assisted automation,often rely heavily on expert knowledge,incur high labor costs,and perform poorly under sparse-data conditions.To address these challenges,this work proposes an Energy Forecasting Large Language Model (EF-LLM),which integrates domain knowledge and temporal data for time-series forecasting while supporting both pre-forecast operations and post-forecast decision-making.EF-LLM's human-artificial intelligence (AI) interaction capabilities further lower the entry barrier for forecasting tasks and reduce the need for additional expert involvement.To support these functions, a continual learning framework with updatable Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and a multi-channel architecture is developed to align heterogeneous multimodal data, enabling EF-LLM to continuously acquire and adapt multimodal knowledge.In addition,EF-LLM improves prediction accuracy under sparse-data conditions by leveraging its ability to process and fuse multimodal information.A Fusion Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (F-PEFT) method is further introduced to effectively exploit both time-series data and textual information.EF-LLM is also the first energy-specific LLM to detect hallucinations and quantify their occurrence rate, achieved via multi-task learning and semantic similarity analysis.This work has achieved success in energy prediction scenarios for load, PV, and wind power forecast.</p></details> |  |
