However, standard KV caches are context-dependent: reusing a cached document in a new context requires recomputing KV states to account for shifts in attention distribution. Existing solutions such as CacheBlend, EPIC, and SAM-KV mitigate this issue by selectively recomputing a subset of tokens; however, they still incur non-negligible computational overhead (FLOPs) and increased Time-to-First-Token (TTFT) latency. In this paper, we propose KV Packet, a recomputation-free cache reuse framework that treats cached documents as immutable "packets" wrapped in light-weight trainable soft-token adapters, which are trained via self-supervised distillation to bridge context discontinuities. Experiments on Llama-3.1 and Qwen-3 demonstrate that the proposed KV Packet method achieves near-zero FLOPs and lower TTFT than recomputation-based baselines, while retaining F1 scores comparable to those of the full recomputation baseline. Code and experimental results are available at https://github.com/ChuangtaoChen-TUM/KVPacket.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 18 pages, including references and appendices. Updated to the camera-ready content with additional experiments</p></details> |
| **[Repairing Broken Access Control in Web Applications with LLMs: An Empirical Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34290v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Broken access control (BAC) ranks among the most severe web-application vulnerability classes, yet its automated repair remains largely unexplored. Unlike memory-safety vulnerabilities, BAC is a logic vulnerability whose repair demands a deep understanding of an application's access-control model and business semantics, which traditional automated vulnerability repair techniques handle poorly. Large language models (LLMs) open a promising avenue for this problem, but no standard benchmark exists for studying how well they repair BAC vulnerabilities. We construct BAC-Bench, the first benchmark for BAC repair, consisting of 46 real-world BAC vulnerabilities drawn from six widely used web applications spanning four programming languages. Each vulnerability is equipped with a sandboxed runtime environment, a PoC validation program and a functional test program. We further propose a BAC repair-strategy framework that characterizes any BAC patch along four dimensions, Position, Coverage, Mechanism, and Content. Evaluating eight base LLMs and two commercial LLM agents on BAC-Bench, we find that single-turn base LLMs repair 37.68% of the vulnerabilities on average, while two minimal scaffolds, Apply-Feedback and Navigate-and-Locate, raise this average to 66.03%, with the best base LLM reaching 75.36%, close to the 78.99% of the best agent. Notably, some LLM-generated patches surpass the official patches, and one incidentally closed a zero-day vulnerability that had lain dormant for three years. Our findings delineate the current capability boundary of LLM-based BAC repair and offer guidance toward its practical adoption.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Russian, a Brazilian, and a German Walk into a Bar, Ask the Same Question-- And Get Three Different Answers: Profiling LLM Perspective Diversity on Contested Topics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34278v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models(LLMs) have shown strong performance on tasks with verifiable answers, but many real-world queries involve subjectivity, disagreement, and conflicting evidence. Most existing benchmarks for such queries remain English-centric, overlooking the vast diversity of perspectives held by users worldwide. To systematically study how LLM perspectives vary across languages, we curate PerspectivesQA, a manually verified multilingual dataset of globally contested topics covering 18 languages, and propose ROOTS, a framework that profiles linguistic, perspective, and framing diversity. We evaluate a diverse set of eight models covering both open-source and proprietary families at varying scales, establishing that LLMs hedge their answers in English but commit to positions in lower-resource languages. Larger models maintain a more consistent cross-lingual worldview, while smaller models are more easily swayed. All models also exhibit high sensitivity to minor surface-level input variations, producing notable stance even in larger models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evaluating LLM-based Agents for Multi-Turn Conversations: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22458v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This survey examines evaluation methods for large language model (LLM)-based agents in multi-turn conversational settings. Using a PRISMA-inspired framework, we systematically reviewed nearly 250 scholarly sources, capturing the state of the art from various venues of publication, and establishing a solid foundation for our analysis. Our study offers a structured approach by developing two interrelated taxonomy systems: one that defines \emph{what to evaluate} and another that explains \emph{how to evaluate}. The first taxonomy identifies key components of LLM-based agents for multi-turn conversations and their evaluation dimensions, including task completion, response quality, user experience, memory and context retention, as well as planning and tool integration. These components ensure that the performance of conversational agents is assessed in a holistic and meaningful manner. The second taxonomy system focuses on the evaluation methodologies. It categorizes approaches into annotation-based evaluations, automated metrics, hybrid strategies that combine human assessments with quantitative measures, and self-judging methods utilizing LLMs. This framework not only captures traditional metrics derived from language understanding, such as BLEU and ROUGE scores, but also incorporates advanced techniques that reflect the dynamic, interactive nature of multi-turn dialogues.</p></details> | <details><summary>Compa...</summary><p>Companion repository: https://github.com/Damon-GSY/multi-turn-agent-evaluation-survey</p></details> |
| **[Honesty Testing of LLMs and AI Agents: A Vision for Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34239v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human oversight of large language models (LLMs) and artificial intelligence (AI) agents increasingly depends on their reports. Claims that conflict with a system's own judgments can conceal failures, prevent timely intervention, and contribute to severe harm. Greater autonomy makes faithful reporting increasingly important for maintaining human control. In this vision paper, we propose honesty testing of LLMs and AI agents as a new research area in software engineering. Building on existing honesty evaluations, our goal is to make faithful reporting a software quality requirement that developers can specify, test, and maintain. We define honesty bugs as behavioural defects in which an LLM or AI agent, when required to communicate faithfully, makes an explicit claim that conflicts with its own judgment at the time of reporting. We present an initial framework covering LLM-level testing under controlled inputs and agent-level testing of complete systems. The framework connects these levels to testing workflows and oracle evidence. We identify research directions in benchmarks, model and harness analysis, oracle construction, and lifecycle testing to support effective oversight alongside task correctness.</p></details> | 5 pages, 1 figure |
| **[VerifyMAS: Hypothesis Verification for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17467v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model-driven multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) excel at complex tasks, yet unreliable agents remain a key bottleneck to system-level reliability. Automatic failure attribution is therefore critical, but existing approaches, such as direct prediction of agent-error pairs and agent-first failure attribution, rely on local logs of agents and miss global failures that only manifest over full interaction trajectories, such as cross-step inconsistencies and inter-agent coordination errors. Moreover, directly predicting failures induces a large combinatorial search space, hindering fine-grained attribution. To address these challenges, we propose VerifyMAS, a hypothesis verification framework for agent failure attribution. Instead of directly predicting faulty agents and error types, VerifyMAS formulates and verifies failure hypotheses against full trajectories. This verification-based approach decomposes attribution into trajectory-level error validation and fine-grained agent localization, providing an error-first attribution approach that captures global failure patterns while substantially reducing the search space. We further introduce a hypothesis-based data construction strategy grounded in a structured error taxonomy and fine-tune a specialized LLM verifier model for trajectory-level failure verification and agent attribution. Experiments on Aegis-Bench and Who&When show that VerifyMAS consistently improves diverse backbone models, including open-source Qwen and API-based GPT models, outperforming prior methods without sacrificing inference efficiency for long multi-agent trajectories.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS2026</p></details> |
| **[LiveOption: Evaluating LLM Agents in Structured Option Trading with Nonlinear Payoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33470v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) and multi-agent systems (MAS) have shown promise in financial decision-making, yet existing evaluations focus on equity trading and primarily assess directional prediction, overlooking the structural complexity of derivative markets. Option trading introduces fundamentally different challenges, including nonlinear payoffs and multi-leg strategy construction, requiring structured decisions rather than simple directional bets. We introduce LiveOption, an evaluation framework for LLM-based agents in option trading. LiveOption formulates the problem as structured sequential decision-making under realistic execution and capital constraints, and provides a reproducible environment with standardized interaction protocols. The framework includes three task suites covering portfolio overlays, event-driven earnings trading, and 0DTE intraday trading. We further propose a hierarchical metric suite that evaluates action validity, decision quality, risk characteristics, and outcome-level performance. Experiments show that current agents often fail to achieve competitive returns in most scenarios. LiveOption offers a principled testbed for evaluating structured decision-making beyond outcome-based metrics. Project Website: https://liveoption.github.io/</p></details> |  |
| **[Arapai: An Offline-First LLM Architecture for Adaptive Learning in Low-Connectivity Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03339v8)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence and large language models (LLMs) are transforming educational technology by enabling conversational tutoring, personalised explanations, and inquiry-driven learning. However, most AI-based learning systems rely on continuous internet connectivity and cloud-based computation, limiting their use in bandwidth-constrained environments. This paper presents Arapai, an offline-first large language model architecture designed for AI-assisted learning in low-connectivity settings. The system performs all inference locally using quantized language models and incorporates hardware-aware model selection to enable deployment on low-specification, CPU-only devices. By removing dependence on cloud infrastructure, the system provides curriculum-aligned explanations and structured academic support through natural-language interaction. To support learners at different educational stages, the system includes adaptive response levels that generate explanations at varying levels of complexity: Simple English, Lower Secondary, Upper Secondary, and Technical. The system was evaluated with 120 students and 9 instructors from secondary and tertiary institutions under limited-connectivity conditions. Results indicate stable operation on legacy hardware, acceptable response times of 1-3 seconds for typical queries, and positive user perceptions of its effectiveness in supporting self-directed learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>There...</summary><p>There is an error in the section IV which state that Arapai was evaluated with 120 students and 9 instructors from secondary and tertiary institutions under limited connectivity conditions. The numbers of 9 instructors and 120 students are not correct</p></details> |
| **[Difference-in-Differences on a Censored Rating Scale Can Manufacture an Effect: Evidence from a Pre-Registered LLM-Judge Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27309v5)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-judge audits assess bias by comparing ratings across matched conditions. Difference-in-differences designs take the rating difference between two candidate responses to each item and compare it across a manipulated attribute. Building on prior work on nonlinear difference-in-differences, we show in closed form that when ratings are bounded, a severity shift common to both responses produces an observed interaction whenever the scale bounds attenuate it unequally. We then report a pre-registered audit of 990 calls to a frozen pedagogy judge. The registered primary analysis did not detect an effect of a stated novice or advanced profile on scaffolding preference. The only nominally significant secondary interaction was on the productive-struggle sub-score ($+0.378$ points; $p = 0.002$). A post-hoc construction with zero differential preference reproduced 79 to 85% of this interaction using the observed severity shift and the scale floor alone. The observed interaction therefore does not identify differential preference on the latent scale.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NewInML Workshop at NeurIPS 2026. Code available at https://github.com/bydeng01/ability-levels-audit</p></details> |
| **[Bug Severity Classification with Fine-Tuned Open LLMs: A Paired Comparison with TF-IDF and CodeBERT Baselines on Eclipse Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34127v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open large language models (LLMs) small enough to run on a laptop are an obvious candidate for automatic bug severity prediction. We measure what they add over cheaper classifiers on one task: assigning one of four Bugzilla severity levels (Critical, Major, Minor, Trivial) to Eclipse bug reports. From a public dump of 361,006 unique Eclipse reports we draw a class-balanced sample of 20,000 rows and compare eleven configurations of nine model types: six TF-IDF classifiers, a fine-tuned CodeBERT encoder, and Llama 3 8B and Qwen 2.5 7B, each run zero-shot and after QLoRA fine-tuning. All eleven are scored on the same 2,000 held-out reports, with bootstrap intervals and McNemar tests. The zero-shot runs reach macro-F1 0.305 and 0.321, below every TF-IDF model (0.430-0.489). The fine-tuned runs reach 0.526 and 0.485, but the zero-shot and fine-tuned setups also differ in checkpoint, serving runtime and decoding, so this gap cannot be credited to fine-tuning alone. Fine-tuned Llama 3 shows no detectable difference from CodeBERT (0.518; difference +0.007, 95% CI -0.017 to +0.032), and fine-tuned Qwen 2.5 is below it on macro-F1. The models differ more by class than in aggregate: fine-tuned Llama 3 finds 36% of Critical reports, Qwen 2.5 finds 69% but only 21% of Minor ones. The dump repeats 60,000 rows, and 8% of the test reports duplicate a training report; removing them lowers TF-IDF scores by up to 0.031 and leaves the LLMs almost unchanged. We release split identifiers, per-report predictions and the analysis script at https://github.com/tbayan/bug_finetuning_llm_artifacts, together with CyberTriage, a tool that serves the fine-tuned classifier locally.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures, 8 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/tbayan/bug_finetuning_llm_artifacts</p></details> |
| **[Human-LLM Alignment in Language Attitudes Toward Non-Native Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01629v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly evaluate human writing in high-stakes domains such as hiring and academic assessment, putting non-native speakers at particular risk. Drawing on the language attitudes framework, we compared human and LLM evaluations of parallel L1- and L2-written Japanese emails on three dimensions: fluency, status, and solidarity. Japanese raters rated L2 texts significantly lower on all three dimensions, with a fluency gap roughly twice the size of the status and solidarity gaps. Six LLM judges reproduced the direction of this bias, and five reproduced its ordering across dimensions. The models diverged from humans in two ways: all understated the solidarity gap, the most socially grounded dimension, and all differentiated among learner L1 backgrounds where humans did not. LLM judges thus reproduce native speakers' language attitudes in a structured yet attenuated form, and the language attitudes framework offers a ready-made yardstick for auditing them beyond English.</p></details> | <details><summary>Withd...</summary><p>Withdrawn: our stimuli, described as emails written by L2 Japanese learners, are in fact Japanese literal translations (I-JAS FOLAS corpus) of emails native speakers wrote in their own languages. The stimuli are not L2 writing, so the paper's title and conclusions about learner writing cannot be sustained. We apologize to readers</p></details> |
| **[Automatic Configuration of LLM Post-Training Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18773v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM post-training pipelines that combine supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning are difficult to configure under realistic compute budgets: the configuration space is high-dimensional and heterogeneous, stages are strongly coupled, and each end-to-end evaluation is expensive. We propose AutoPipe, a budget-aware two-phase framework for configuration selection in LLM post-training. Offline, AutoPipe learns a dataset-conditioned learning-to-rank surrogate from historical runs, capturing within-dataset preferences and providing transferable guidance toward promising regions of the configuration space. Online, for a new dataset, AutoPipe uses the offline guidance to steer Bayesian optimization and models dataset-specific deviations with a Gaussian-process residual surrogate. To reduce evaluation cost, each trial is early-stopped and scored by a learned predictor that maps early training signals to a low-cost proxy for final post-training performance. Experiments on biomedical reasoning tasks show that AutoPipe achieves performance comparable to full-fidelity HPO baselines at a fraction of their online cost, while outperforming offline-only approaches and early-stopped HPO variants.</p></details> |  |
| **[The LALA Benchmark: Evaluating Literary and Linguistic Analysis in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34080v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce the Literature and Linguistics Analysis Benchmark (LALA), comprising 236 open-ended questions written by experts in linguistics and literary studies, together with expert-provided ideal answers. By comparing models within families and at similar training scales using human and LLM judges, LALA identifies differences in linguistic competence associated with post-training, offering a framework for evaluating future models and guiding targeted improvements. We administered questions to 17 language models and evaluated their responses using both human expert assessment and two LLM evaluators, Sol and Qwen. Base models displayed little of the linguistic competence assessed by the benchmark, whereas their post-trained versions performed markedly better, suggesting that post-training plays a central role in making linguistic knowledge accessible and usable. Parameter count, by contrast, showed no statistically significant association with partial-credit performance, and competencies remained unevenly distributed across linguistic domains. We interpret this pattern as evidence that post-training adds linguistic competencies piecemeal, without producing a general linguistic intelligence capable of extending them beyond the learned policies that support them. Models performed best on logical relations between statements and selected discourse-level tasks, while even the strongest post-trained models continued to struggle with ambiguity, literary style, reference, and misleading assumptions. Human and Sol evaluations produced nearly identical model rankings in both the original and ReliableEval runs, supporting the replicability of the comparative results.</p></details> | 49 pages, 10 figures |
| **[Terminus-4B: Can a Smaller Model Replace Frontier LLMs at Agentic Execution Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03195v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern coding agents increasingly delegate specialized subtasks to subagents, which are smaller, focused agentic loops that handle narrow responsibilities like search, debugging or terminal execution. This architectural pattern keeps the main agent's context window clean by isolating verbose outputs (e.g. build logs, test results, etc.) within the subagent context. Typically when agents employ subagents for such tasks, they use frontier models as these subagents. In this paper, we investigate whether a finetuned small language model (SLM) can achieve comparable performance to frontier models in the task of agentic terminal execution. We present Terminus-4B, which is a post-trained Qwen3-4B model via Supervised Finetuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) using rubric-based LLM-as-judge reward, specifically for this task. In our extensive evaluation spanning various frontier models, training ablations and main agent configurations, we find that Terminus-4B is able to reduce the token usage of the main agent by up to ~30% compared to the No Subagent baseline with no impact to agent performance on benchmarks like SWE-Bench Pro and our internal SWE-Bench C# benchmark, which tends to be heavy in verbose execution tasks. Furthermore, Terminus-4B improves key metrics showing the main agent relying on the outputs of the subagent and doing fewer terminal execution tasks by itself. We see that our model not only closes the gap between the Vanilla Qwen model and frontier models like Claude Sonnet / Opus / GPT-5.3-Codex, but often even exceeds their performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>The c...</summary><p>The current article involves some product IP issues and needs to be withdrawn and re-approved</p></details> |
